Every path-vacating span (stale break, release) and every path-occupying operation (publication) now runs under a recovery guard — `.quotameter.lock.guard`, acquired by the same atomic scratch+`link(2)` publication as the lock itself — so a publisher can never interleave into a breaker's vacancy (guard acquisition is one unconditional link: no check-then-act window on either side) and the restoring link always finds the path vacant. A guard holder that crashed mid-span is detected by its recorded pid and broken with the same capture-then-decide primitive one level down, and once under the guard any capture aside orphaned by the crash is restored to the path it was displaced from — a live holder's lock now survives even a crash of the process that was recovering it. The lock payload `{"pid":N,"id":<token>}` is byte-unchanged (the guard is an additional internal file); all D-017 invariants (no false-dead break, damaged/foreign content never stomped, token-guarded release, bounded attempts, error messages) are preserved, and the one-level-down guard race that cannot be closed without infinite regress is documented as D-086's accepted residual (an orphaned aside is dropped only when the occupied path carries byte-identical content, so even that residual can only strand a file, never destroy a live holder's lock). Two real-child-process regression tests reproduce the reviewer's three-process interleaving (breaker paused after the rename with the path vacant, third starter waiting — observed causally through its first guard-wait poll — and mutual exclusion held: the displaced holder keeps the lock, both intruders exit 1 with the actionable error) and the crash-mid-recovery case; eleven in-process tests pin the guard machinery (live-holder wait, wedged-guard attempt-limit refusal, unparseable/dangling guard, orphan-aside restoration, redundant-aside cleanup and divergent-aside preservation, mid-release retake, live-guard capture residual, release abandonment, capture-failure propagation).

- The credential-validation suite no longer races its own deadline under coverage load (U-012, spec §7.2, §17.1; decision D-087): every test in `test/integration/zai-coding-plan-validate.spec.ts` shared one `timeoutMs: 120` — §7.2's total deadline (D-042), which starts inside `providerFetch` before the request is even sent and bounds DNS, connect, headers, and the full body read — so each delivered-response assertion silently required the whole loopback exchange to finish within 120 ms of runner time. Under v8 coverage instrumentation (permanent in `make check` since task 125, D-085) plus parallel-suite load that bound was exceeded once (2026-10-31, task 125's full run in the t-125 worktree: "resolves on 200 with the /models stand-in body" failed with `provider request timed out after 120 ms`; green in isolation 11/11 and in two full re-runs — the U-007/U-010 load-sensitivity family; a task-120 reviewer had observed the same suite flake once). The transport classified correctly throughout; the defect was the test's coupling to runner speed. Delivered-response tests now run at `DELIVERED_RESPONSE_TIMEOUT_MS = 5_000` (the transport suite's value for the same mock round-trip since task 104, and §7.2's minimum configured timeout), while the deadline-expiry test keeps the short 120 ms deadline against its `10 × TIMEOUT_MS` response delay — a margin load can only widen, since the mock's delay timer starts at request arrival, strictly after the deadline. Verified: 10/10 suite runs in isolation under coverage, 10/10 under deliberate CPU starvation (six busy-loop hogs on the 8-core container, the U-010 method), and four full `make check` runs at ambient parallel-worktree load (load average 11–22): the validate suite green in all four, three runs fully green (104 files / 2030 tests), and the one remaining failure — `test/unit/history-downsample.property.spec.ts` exceeding Vitest's default 5 s per-test timeout at load ~18 — is a pre-existing, out-of-scope property-suite sensitivity flagged for follow-up, not a deadline race. The mock server (`test/helpers/mockProviderServer.ts`) was investigated and is not implicated; no production code changed.
- A component whose rollback stop rejected is no longer leaked with no retry route (U-018, spec §5.6, §17.1; decision D-088): `QuotameterApp`'s startup rollback stops the started components best-effort and swallows per-component stop failures so the original startup error can propagate — which left a component whose stop rejected (an open SQLite handle, a listener, a timer) holding its resource forever: the state became `'failed'` and `stop()` was documented as a no-op from there, contradicting `start()`'s own "leaves nothing running" contract and letting a leaked handle keep the process alive after `runServer` had returned its failure code. The rollback now records exactly the components it could not release, and `stop()` from `'failed'` retries exactly those, in reverse registration order (D-020's stop-order semantics untouched), never re-stopping ones the rollback or an earlier retry already released. A fully successful retry ends at `'stopped'`; a failure surfaces the shutdown vocabulary's `AggregateError` (`'one or more components failed to stop'`, carrying that round's errors only — the original startup error still propagates from `start()`), the still-unreleased components stay recorded, and the state stays `'failed'` with `stopPromise` cleared, so cleanup is repeatable until every resource releases (concurrent calls coalesce into the in-flight retry; a rollback that released everything keeps `stop()` a no-op). The `LifecycleComponent` contract now states that `stop` may be re-called after its own rejection, and both docstrings — `start()`'s and `stop()`'s state table — describe the implemented semantics. `test/integration/server-app.spec.ts` proves the done-condition with a release-marker component: a rejected rollback stop leaves the marker held, one `stop()` retry releases it while the successfully rolled-back sibling is never stopped again, a permanently failing stop yields the `AggregateError` and stays retryable across rounds until it releases, and concurrent retries coalesce; the existing server-app and server-main tests are unmodified and green (38 combined).
- The soft-endpoint abort test no longer races the primary exchange (U-010): `test/integration/zai-coding-plan-snapshot.spec.ts`'s "an abort during a soft endpoint degrades the cycle, never fails it" gated its caller abort on the arrival of the **first** request — the primary's — at the mock server, but the primary exchange completes only when the client has read the response, and under full-suite parallel load the abort could land inside that arrival-to-read window, where `TransportAbortError` propagation out of `fetchSnapshot` is the designed §8.1 behavior (observed once, in the first full `make check-all` after task 113's landing; green in isolation and on immediate re-run — the same family as U-007 but a test phase-assumption race, not a product hole). The gate is now causal, not timing-based: the cycle's exchanges are strictly sequential (endpoint 1 → parse → endpoint 2), so the test waits for the **subscription request** to arrive — proof the primary completed client-side — before aborting, and the abort can only ever hit the soft endpoints. Verified under deliberate CPU starvation (six busy-loop hogs on the 8-core container): 10/10 snapshot-suite runs, 3/3 server-main runs, and two full `make check-all` runs (one under load, one clean) all green. The same investigation explained and removed the `quotameter: one or more components failed to stop: broken stop` noise line seen in full runs: the "component fails to stop" test fires its SIGTERM manually but left its trigger in the suite's `afterEach` safety net, whose second shutdown ran **after** `captureStderr` restored and printed the deliberately-injected fatal line to the real stderr — the consumed trigger is now removed and the line is gone (0/3 runs). The two clean-shutdown tests sharing the fire-and-leave-the-trigger pattern are left as-is on purpose: their second shutdown is silent and harmless (three counting the signal-readiness handshake test).
- The transport no longer resolves a deadline-exceeded exchange as a success (U-007, spec §7.2; decision D-072): `providerFetch`'s body-read loop treated a `done` stream resolution as success without consulting the §7.2 deadline — D-042's "signal state is the classification authority" rule was enforced only on the rejection paths. Under a rare abort-vs-read race on a loaded 2-core runner (CI run 41162981449, matrix job `test (22.13.0)`, "times out while reading a slow body"), undici closed the aborted trickle body as `done`, so an exchange that had blown its 120 ms deadline resolved as `{ status: 200, … }`, possibly truncated — which the outcome mapping would have recorded as a parse failure (`Format changed`) instead of the timeout (`Unreachable`) it was. Both success returns — the read loop's `done` exit and the bodiless-2xx return — now verify the deadline and caller signal state first, through one shared classifier whose deadline-wins precedence now lives (and is test-pinned) in a single place: a fired deadline yields `TransportTimeoutError`, a fired caller signal `TransportAbortError`. Delivered non-2xx, wrong-content-type, and over-cap responses keep their classifications even when a signal raced them — an arrived status is more actionable, and §7.2's 429 and 401/403 rows must not collapse into the retryable timeout row on a timer race. The lost race is pinned deterministically socket-free through the injected `fetchImpl` (a fetch double that resolves only after the composed signal aborted): done-resolving bodies (empty and truncated) and bodiless 2xx responses map to the timeout/abort errors, a clean completion still succeeds, both-fired keeps the deadline winning, and the observation paths keep their classes; the real-socket suites are unchanged and green.
- Non-finite configuration numbers are named truthfully in field-level errors (task 113, spec §9.2; decision D-071): a numeric setting arriving as `Infinity`, `-Infinity`, or `NaN` — reachable from a hand-edited file through an out-of-range JSON literal such as `1e400`, which strict `JSON.parse` yields as `Infinity` — was already rejected by both validation engines (TypeBox and the Ajv-compiled contract exclude non-finite doubles from the `number` type, verified on the pinned runtime), but the rendered problem contradicted itself (`multiplier: expected a number, got a number`). Type errors now say `got a non-finite number` for every numeric setting, remaining value-free per §15.4; task 067's pinned expectation for its env digit-string route into `server.port` is updated to the repaired wording.
- Z.ai metric derivations stay finite (task 101, spec §8.4; decision D-057): an arithmetic derivation (`remaining = limit − used`, `used = limit − remaining`, `limit = used + remaining`, `percentUsed = 100 · used / limit`) or the `windowSeconds = number × 3600` scaling whose result overflows IEEE-754 finiteness (extreme magnitudes; zero allowances in a quotient) now leaves the field absent and unlisted instead of emitting `Infinity`/`NaN` values the shared §8.4 snapshot invariants reject — the supplied values survive and the metric stays valid. Zero-allowance behavior from task 100's review is unchanged: result-finiteness subsumes the old `limit !== 0` guard.
- The rolling mutation-report issue no longer shows a self-contradictory 0% report: the renderer (extracted to `scripts/render-mutation-report.mjs`, now callable by the workflow and by hand against any artifact JSON) normalizes Stryker's PascalCase mutant statuses, so the score, per-file table, and survived triage list match the report JSON, with runtime/compile-error mutants surfaced as counts and files (U-005, decision D-054).
- The daily mutation workflow now bootstraps its `mutation-report` label idempotently before first use: `gh issue create --label` requires the label to already exist and cannot create it, which failed the workflow's validation run at the issue-upsert step (U-004).
- `npm audit` restored after the npm registry retired the quick-audit endpoint: the dev image and CI now pin npm 11 (bulk advisory endpoint) while Node stays at the 22.13.0 minimum.

### Added

- The time-zone regression matrix runs in CI (task 126, spec §17.1; decision D-097): §17.1 requires the time-zone suites to "run under different `TZ` values", but no CI leg ever set `TZ` — every suite ran under the runner's default zone, so the `TZ`-invariance D-021 (zone-explicit branded clock API) and D-073 (pinned-literal epochs) built was verified only by local ad-hoc runs. A new `timezone` job in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (behind the required `CI` gate) runs the time/peak-hours suite — `npm run test:time`, a new script selecting the twelve time and peak-hours spec files (clock, compare-epoch-ms, rolling-ranges ×3 including the task-116 child-process suite, and the six peak-hours suites) through Vitest CLI substring filters — once per process `TZ` matrix cell: the seven §17.1-required identifiers (`UTC`, `America/New_York`, `Europe/London`, `Asia/Shanghai`, `Asia/Kolkata`, `Australia/Lord_Howe`, `Pacific/Chatham`) plus `Asia/Kathmandu` (never-DST quarter-hour — the required list covers quarter hours only through DST Chatham) and `Pacific/Kiritimati` (+14:00 dateline edge, the task-116 precedent), covering DST and non-DST offsets at whole-, half-, and quarter-hour granularity. Non-vacuity is enforced, not hoped for: `scripts/verify-tz.mjs` runs in each cell before the suite — in the fresh process, the task-116 discipline — and verifies that the default zone `Intl` resolved matches the expected canonical name (ICU canonicalizes `Asia/Kolkata` → `Asia/Calcutta`, `Asia/Kathmandu` → `Asia/Katmandu`) and that the zone's UTC offset at two fixed probes (2026-01-15, 2026-07-15), parsed from `longOffset` and compared **numerically**, equals the recorded minute-exact DST/fractional-offset facts — numeric because ICU spells a zero offset `GMT` on Node 22.13.0 but `GMT+00:00` on Node 24, and a display-string table made the guard fail UTC and Europe/London's winter probe on Node 24 (the review's reproduced P1: the main test matrix spawns the guard through the integration spec on Node 22.13.0/22/24) — so a CI wiring bug collapsing every cell to one zone, or a tzdata that lost a zone, fails loudly instead of passing vacuously; the job pins Node 22.13.0 (the canonical-name expectations were generated from that runtime's ICU) while the runtime dimension stays with the main `test` matrix. The selection is an executable contract: `test/unit/time-suite-manifest.spec.ts` parses `test:time` from package.json, applies the filters to the real test trees with the same substring-OR rule (verified against the installed Vitest 5.0.5), and requires the selection to equal the pinned manifest — a time/peak-hours spec renamed outside the filters or a dead filter fails `make check`; a spec added outside the filter vocabulary is the documented blind spot (no structural signal says a file is "time/peak-hours" besides its module-named filename — D-097). `test/integration/tz-verify-script.spec.ts` pins the guard's own contract (all nine zones accepted with the zone echoed, unset/empty `TZ` rejected naming the fresh-process rule, a valid non-matrix zone rejected listing the nine) and the zone membership on both remaining sides — a line scanner compares the workflow's `timezone` job matrix with the same pinned list, so a zone dropped from the workflow or the script's table fails the suite. `make test-time [ZONE=<zone>]` reproduces any cell locally (the guard runs first under `ZONE`); `make test-time-matrix` walks all nine cells and is wired into `make check-all`, keeping its documented full CI parity; the subset runs without coverage — thresholds are judged once by `test:ci` in the main job, the matrix adds environmental repetition, not coverage. Verified: the full matrix (all nine zones, guard + suite) green in the container on Node 22.13.0 and the guard green on all nine zones on Node 24.14.0 (both zero-offset spellings accepted), plus a scratch filter-rename demonstration failing the manifest guard in both directions.
- The coverage-threshold map can no longer be silently orphaned by renames (U-015, spec §17.3; decision D-093): a zero-match key in `vitest.config.ts`'s `coverage.thresholds` passes silently — every file key in this repo's config is `perFile`, and the installed Vitest (5.0.5; verified in `resolveThresholds`/`checkThresholds`) judges exactly the files a key matches, so a dead key judges nothing and renaming a module family (`peak-hours*`) or an exact-path module without updating the config would leave a 100% entry enforcing nothing while `make check` stays green. The new `test/unit/coverage-thresholds.spec.ts` — the `package-layout.spec.ts`/`storage-sql-gate.spec.ts` structural pattern, task 125's review P3-1 which that task could not add (sibling-owned suites during its wave) — imports the config by module import (same-process truth, no text parsing of a TypeScript file) and pins three contracts (D-093): every non-global threshold key matches at least one existing `src/**` `.ts` file; every module of the §17.3 critical map (D-085 plus D-089/U-016's four — clock, rolling-ranges, the `peak-hours*` family, sanitize, logger, permissions, write, transport, env, load, reload) is matched by some key, so a config regression that drops or narrows a key fails; and the map stays fresh — a pattern that matches no src file means a §17.3 critical module moved or disappeared, failing with instructions to update the map and the config key together. Matching mirrors the installed Vitest one-for-one — `picomatch` with default options against repo-root-relative posix paths, the same library and call shape `resolveThresholds` uses — with a positive control pinning the relied-on semantics (a prefix glob matches no sibling outside the family, `*` never crosses `/`, exact paths are literal) and shape controls pinning the object form of `thresholds`, the `coverage.include` universe, and the presence of the four global threshold axes (§17.3/D-085's aggregate layer — presence is structure, values stay policy). Verified in both directions: green on the real config, and scratch runs (not committed) — with the family key renamed to `peak-windows*.ts`, the exact `npm run test:ci` command `make check` runs exits 0 with the dead key present (2067 tests green, zero threshold errors) and exits non-zero once the guard is in place, naming the orphaned key and both unenforced family files; `git mv`-renaming both family files fails the targeted guard run the same way; deleting the `clock.ts` key line fails it with the uncovered-module message. Deliberate limit (recorded in D-093): a single family member renamed out of the glob is not caught — no structural signal distinguishes a rename from a removal — and threshold values (D-089's branch maxima) are coverage policy, out of the guard's scope.
- Event and history property coverage (task 124, spec §12, §13, §17.1; decision D-084): the five landed §12 suites are extended additively and two new property suites close their gaps. `test/unit/history-cross.property.spec.ts` proves the modules compose — a generated fixed-period series runs through the actual D-074 reset detector and D-075 gap detector and their outputs feed the D-081 downsampler as boundary data, where every gap's edge points survive as bucket endpoints (a bucket ending at `fromEpochMs`, one starting at `toEpochMs`) and every reset instant falls strictly between consecutive buckets; a second property drives D-075 and task 120's D-080 timeline indicators from one shared run history (metric points at successful run starts, constant cadence) and pins §12.2's consumer promise: every stopped-service region straddled by observed points is covered by a collection gap, and every collection gap is explained — it contains a stopped region or at least one failed-cycle error indicator, so a chart line never breaks invisibly (cycle durations are bounded far below the silence margin, keeping the two thresholds' different inputs from skewing; the skew window itself is unpromised, see D-084). `test/unit/rolling-ranges.property.spec.ts` pins the §10 range invariants over generated instants across the whole ECMAScript epoch-millisecond range — exact closed bounds ending at `now`, ±1 ms exclusions, and the nesting of the five §14.4 ranges. The events suite gains the §12.1 condition-1 biconditional (an event fires exactly when an independent table oracle fires — evidence plus no contradicting pair — with exactly its reason codes in `RESET_SIGNAL_CODES` canonical order, tolerance edges tripwired: a one-point percentage move is neither evidence nor veto, a one-unit absolute move is always decisive) and the event-time biconditional over generated declaration placements (both `previous_resets_at` and `current_collected_at` stamping, for value-only events too — the previously untested interaction — plus both conditions firing together), and a generator-growth fold oracle (80-point interleavings with equal-timestamp clusters and double-run determinism). The gaps suite gains an interval-change-timeline oracle: generated multi-change timelines judged against brute-force D-075 span-widening with full-record equality, gap-record self-consistency, and a tripwire proving the widened-cadence path runs. The downsampling suite gains equal-timestamp clusters under boundaries (every stamp-sharing group lands in exactly one bucket; boundaries landing on cluster stamps), the degenerate zero-span overfull series (exactly one bucket, `resolutionMs: 0`, first/last identity), and generator growth to §13's budget ceilings (400-point series against budgets up to 5000, full preservation checks). All additions use the shared `test/helpers/property` convention (100 runs, `FC_SEED` reproduction, no clock reads — D-021) and ship with vacuity tripwires; no existing assertion was weakened and no production code changed. Non-flakiness evidence: three full-suite runs and three isolated `--sequence.shuffle` runs of the seven suites all green, plus one `FC_SEED`-pinned run.
- Explicit §9.3 DST resolution for schedule local times (task 115, spec §9.3, §17.1; decision D-079) in `src/services/peak-hours-dst.ts`: `resolveLocalInstantDst` is the authoritative — and now default — `LocalInstantResolver`: nonexistent local readings (inside a spring-forward gap) shift forward by the **exact size of the gap** (the offset delta — one hour in New York, 30 minutes in `Australia/Lord_Howe`, a gap starting at 02:45 in `Pacific/Chatham`, midnight in `America/Havana`), and ambiguous readings (inside a fall-back overlap) resolve to the **first occurrence**. The policy is computed by offset differencing through the canonical D-021 epoch→zone bridge (`zonedParts`): candidate offsets probed a day around the reading, occurrences verified by exact wall-clock round-trip, first occurrence = the earliest verified instant, gap = the pre-transition offset's instant — not delegated to Luxon's offset-guess convergence (which lands on the same answers today but is not a contract; the retained compat resolver `resolveLocalInstantLuxon` is pinned equal across the matrix except one documented divergence: its `24:00` is shifted-midnight plus a calendar day, which double-shifts when the day's own midnight was gapped — e.g. `24:00` of Havana's 2026-03-08). Because §9.3's gap shift is **not monotone** in wall order (a nonexistent 02:30 resolves past the real 03:00), two wall-disjoint §9.2-valid windows can cover overlapping instants — reachable through the landed default — so `calculatePeakHoursIntervals` now emits the **union** of peak occurrences: candidates sorted by `(start, end)`, each start clipped to the still-uncovered stretch (local times rendered from the finally emitted boundaries), keeping the D-073 partition contract — chronological, adjacent, single coverage — true for any resolver; a window whose edges meet or invert (a start inside a gap whose shift passes its end) still collapses to off-peak. Done-condition proof: `test/unit/peak-hours-dst.spec.ts` pins gap/ambiguity edges by hand-derived exact epochs for every §17.1 zone (plus Havana's midnight gap and the production zone `Asia/Singapore`), `test/unit/peak-hours-dst.matrix.spec.ts` walks whole timelines across the matrix (23/25-hour days, windows inside/across gaps and overlaps, `24:00` ends through transitions), and `test/unit/peak-hours-dst.property.spec.ts` checks **every minute** of every transition day (and neighbors) against an independent brute-force scan oracle, Luxon parity included, plus fast-check properties (fixed-offset zones never shift; arbitrary 2024–2028 readings agree with the oracle); the suites were verified green under six host `TZ` values. Both peak-hours modules hold 100% statement/branch/function/line scoped coverage (§17.3).
- Timeline indicators for collection errors and downtime (spec §12.2, §13) in `src/services/history-timeline.ts` (task 120, decision D-080): `buildTimelineIndicators(runs, range)` is the pure second half of §12.2, complementary to task 119's metric-point gaps — run records plus an explicit query range go in, a `kind`-discriminated indicator union comes out, ready for `GET /api/events`. Failed collection cycles (`collection_runs` rows with a non-success outcome) each yield one `collection_error` indicator typed by the §11.2 outcome vocabulary (D-048/D-034, imported from `polling-outcomes.ts`, never redefined) and carrying the run's evidence — HTTP status, sanitized error message, §7.2 attempt count, deferred-429 `retryAfterSeconds` — positioned at the cycle's own start/end span, so the chart's per-error-type symbols and tooltips (§14.4's lower timeline strip) need no second lookup; adjacent cycles of distinct failure types never merge. Intervals with **no run record at all** (success or failure) yield `stopped_service` regions — the hatched "service was stopped" areas — for maximal run-less sub-intervals of the range whose silence strictly exceeds the same three jitter-expanded effective intervals task 119 pinned (`gapThresholdMs` imported from `history-gaps.ts`: one source of truth), judged by the more permissive of the two boundary records' own per-run interval stamps (D-075's span-widening; a mid-silence §6.4 change re-anchors the schedule, and the fail-safe direction is no region) and clamped to the range edges, where the leading/trailing silence is anchored by the first/last record the caller supplies — records just outside the range give exact causal anchoring. The two classes are structurally distinguishable — the task's done-condition — through the discriminant: error indicators carry an `outcome` from the failure vocabulary, stopped indicators carry none at all (nothing was collected, so there is no failure type); both the runtime separation and the type-level narrowing are pinned by the closing spec block. Retry-After deferrals and auth-suspension probe gaps are deliberately not subtracted from the record-based class (D-075's precedent keeps scheduler policy gates out of the §12.2 history view; the deferral evidence travels on the boundary runs). Accounts partition independently (first-appearance order, chronological within an account — a region always precedes the record that closes it), input order is irrelevant, empty input answers `[]`, and every malformed input fails fast with a caller-addressed English message (§7.1 interval bounds against the scheduler's own constants, unknown outcomes, inverted ranges and cycles, §7.2's attempt and Retry-After ranges). Verified by `test/unit/history-timeline.spec.ts` (all six failure types, adjacent distinct types, the threshold boundary on both sides, split outages, both range edges with clamping, boundary-cadence widening in both directions, overlapping records and the aliveness frontier, per-account isolation, unsorted and duplicate-timestamp input, the degenerate ranges, the full validation table, and the executable done-condition) and `test/unit/history-timeline.property.spec.ts` (error indicators are exactly the failed cycles, stopped regions are disjoint/in-range/past-their-threshold/record-free, and the output is invariant under input permutation up to the account-block order — with vacuity tripwires).
- History series downsampling (spec §13, §17.1; task 121, decision D-081) in `src/services/history-downsample.ts`: `downsampleHistorySeries(points, maxPoints, boundaries?)` aggregates an overfull metric series (more points than §13's `maxPoints` — default 1500, accepted 100–5000, both pinned as exported constants) into time buckets that each preserve their per-field minima and maxima (`used`, `remaining`, `percentUsed`, `percentRemaining` over the members that carry each field; a field absent from every member is absent from the extrema) and their first and last member points — the caller's own objects, by reference. No bucket ever aggregates across a critical boundary: reset instants (D-074 `ResetEvent.eventAt`, consumed as data) separate the pair they land in — a reset stamped at a point's own collection time (`current_collected_at`) groups that point with the post side — and gap intervals (D-075 `CollectionGap` edges, as data) separate exactly the pair straddling them, with both edge points keeping their own side of the silence; equal timestamps never separate. Boundaries cut the chronologically sorted series into segments aggregated independently, each apportioned `1 + ⌊nᵢ·E/N⌋` uniform time slices (the arithmetic keeps every allocation ≥ 1, ≤ its segment's point count, and the total within the budget — no capping or remainder passes); slice membership is exact cross-multiplied integer arithmetic, empty slices are skipped, and bucket edges are observed timestamps. §13's "must not be lost" outranks the count target: a series with more segments than the budget (unreachable below 100 boundaries) keeps one bucket per segment and may exceed `maxPoints` — merging across a boundary is the one loss the spec forbids. At or under the budget the series passes through identically (stable chronological order, `resolutionMs: null`); above it, the effective resolution is the widest allocated slice width (`segment span / bucket count`, maximized — observed member spans would understate how coarsely clustered points were merged). Output is deterministic (shuffled input with distinct timestamps yields identical buckets) and frozen; invalid arguments fail fast with caller-addressed messages. The done-condition — property tests verify the preservation rules — is met by `test/unit/history-downsample.property.spec.ts` (bucket-count bounds including the carve-out bound, a chronological member walk that reproduces every bucket's first/last references and count as the coverage/no-loss/no-reorder oracle, per-field extrema equal to the true aggregates of exactly the walked members, whole-series extrema surviving in the bucket extrema, no bucket spanning any reset or gap, the exact passthrough/downsampled mode boundary, determinism and input-order invariance) and `test/unit/history-downsample.spec.ts` (a hand-computed bucket layout, both boundary kinds on both edge-placement variants, out-of-span boundaries, adjacent separations with a one-point segment, simultaneous and clustered series, the dense-boundary carve-out, allocation never exceeding segment size, immutability, and the fail-fast validation table).
- Retention-aware history queries (task 123, spec §11.3, §13; decision D-082) in `src/storage/history-retention.ts`: the query-side twin of D-063's cleanup — `boundHistoryRange(requested, { now, retentionDays })` bounds a requested epoch-ms range to the retention horizon (`now − retentionDays` days, D-063's exact `historyCutoff` arithmetic) and reports one of three outcomes: **ok** (the range lies at or after the horizon; effective bounds unchanged), **truncated** (a partially older range clamped to `[horizon, to]` with the cut `requestedFrom` reported), or **rejected** (the entire range precedes the horizon — returned as data with the horizon and requested bounds, not a throw, so the §13 layer can state it in the response whichever way the spec's "reject or explicitly truncate" is wired). `getMetricSeriesWithRetention` / `listQuotaEventsWithRetention` / `listCollectionRunsWithRetention` serve the already-clamped reads (§13's range-bearing endpoints' storage sources) beside the report as `{ range, rows }`, empty rows on rejection, facade filters (metric key, run outcome) passing through. Only the lower bound clamps (`to` passes through untouched); the bounds share the landed reads' closed-interval convention (D-068), and with the same window the clamp and D-063's strict-before deletes describe the same retained set — pinned by running the cleanup and comparing. Out-of-policy rows are never served even between daily cleanups; the raw facade reads stay unclamped (fifth sibling over D-058, wrap-not-modify); §11.3's 7–3650 days and finite, non-inverted bounds are validated before anything is read. `test/integration/history-retention.spec.ts` pins both halves of every read plus every horizon boundary (both sides of `from`/`to`, the point range at the horizon), the validation table, per-account isolation, the hidden-while-undeleted rule, the unclamped facade, the D-063 alignment, and the one bounded divergence (a run started before the horizon whose snapshot collected after it is served until its run deletes).
- Peak-hours interval calculation (spec §9.3, §10, §13; task 114, decision D-073) in `src/services/peak-hours-intervals.ts`: `calculatePeakHoursIntervals(schedule, range, resolver?)` turns a validated `providers.<id>.peakHours` section plus an explicit half-open `[from, to)` query range into the UTC timeline the future `/api/peak-hours` route and the §14 chart highlighting consume — every window occurrence intersecting the range becomes a peak interval carrying exact UTC epoch-millisecond boundaries, the window's id and name, its multiplier, and both boundaries read back as `yyyy-MM-ddTHH:mm` local times in the schedule zone, and the stretches between them become off-peak intervals carrying `offPeakMultiplier`; the intervals partition the whole range chronologically (adjacent, no gaps, no double coverage). The walk runs on local calendar days of the schedule zone starting one guard day before the range's first local day (a `24:00` window reaching only through its final midnight is still considered); it is count-bounded and anchored — each day is one `plus({days: offset})` from the range's first local midnight — because a stepping cursor compared by instant silently drops the range's last local day in zones whose spring-forward gap starts at midnight (`America/Havana` and kin; found in review, pinned by two Havana regressions), `24:00` resolves to the following local midnight, and a window that collapses under the resolver (entirely inside a spring-forward gap) is dropped, its wall-clock span staying off-peak. Local wall-clock → instant conversion — exactly where §9.3's DST policy lives — is an injectable `LocalInstantResolver` seam whose default `resolveLocalInstantLuxon` follows Luxon's documented resolution (nonexistent local times shift forward by the gap, ambiguous ones take the first occurrence; verified empirically for Luxon 3.7.2), so task 115 can pin the §17.1 transition matrix — or replace the default — without touching the interval algorithm (decision D-021's single-siting rule). `from === to` yields an empty timeline; a non-finite or inverted range, a span over 3660 local days (retention's 3650 plus headroom, refusing an unbounded day walk), or a non-IANA zone fails loudly with an English error. The done-condition is verified by the directed matrix (`test/unit/peak-hours-intervals.spec.ts`: the canonical Z.ai week in `Asia/Singapore` pinned by deep equality with exact intervals, names, rates, and local times; mid-window clipping at both ends; half-open boundary exclusions; `24:00` and full-day windows; multi-window days in arbitrary config order; multiplier defaults; every fail-fast path; the seam via a recording and a skewed resolver; DST smoke cases explicitly labeled as the default's observable output) and the property suite (`test/unit/peak-hours-intervals.property.spec.ts`: over generated schedules and ranges in fixed-offset zones, the intervals partition `[from, to)` exactly and every interval's kind agrees with an independent epoch → local classifier built on `zonedParts` — the D-021-unambiguous direction — with vacuity tripwires). The module reads no machine zone or clock, and the suite was verified green under `TZ` = UTC, America/New_York, Pacific/Kiritimati, and Asia/Kathmandu; scoped coverage of the module is 100% statements/branches/functions/lines (spec §17.3's peak-hours target).
- Cycle-persisted reset events with §12.1 timing and evidence (spec §12.1; task 118, decision D-078) in `src/services/events-cycle.ts` and the cycle write path: `detectCycleResetEvents(repository, accountId, snapshot)` is the storage-reading half of task 117's detector, and `createCycleRecorder`'s success write now carries exactly its output through D-060's §11.1 transaction — each **valid** metric of the new snapshot is compared once, pairwise, against the last valid point of its stored (account, metric) series read via `getMetricSeries` **before** the write (so only prior cycles can form the comparison base and detection never re-folds already-persisted history: a stored reset is attributed to the cycle that detected it, exactly once), invalid metrics of the new snapshot are skipped at the seam, and trailing invalid stored rows are walked back past (§12.1's "invalid points do not participate", applied to both sides; an out-of-order snapshot is suppressed by the detector's chronology guard — a missed event, never a false one). The stored row keeps the detector's evidence verbatim — reason in `details.reason` (D-074's vocabulary, `rolling_window_conservative` suffix included), the compared points in `details.previous`/`details.current` with the previous point's `runId` passed through from its stored row while the current point truthfully carries none (the cycle's run id does not exist until the write assigns it; the event row's `run_id` is the writer's injection), plus `tolerance` and `eventTimeSource` — and `event_at` follows §12.1's rule: the previous `resetsAt` when it lay between the two snapshots, else the new collection time. Failed and aborted cycles still store no events (they never reach detection, and the D-046 composite foreign key makes an event-without-its-snapshot row unrepresentable). The done-condition — event timing and evidence match §12.1 — is proven end-to-end against a real `node:sqlite` database by `test/integration/events-cycle.spec.ts`: both event-time branches with full evidence round-trips through the D-046 CHECK contract, the silences (first cycle, steady values, failed and aborted cycles), the invalid-intermediate skip-back to the previous valid point, the no-refold invariant, and per-metric independence; `test/unit/events-cycle.spec.ts` pins the mapping edges (SQL `NULL` → absent fields, walk-back, out-of-order suppression, invalid-metric skip) and the unit polling-persist suite pins the write input.
- Conservative quota-reset-event detection (spec §12.1; task 117, decision D-074) in `src/services/events-detection.ts`: `detectResetEvent(previous, current)` compares one new valid metric point against the previous valid point of the same account-and-metric series and `detectSeriesResetEvents(points)` folds a flat interleaved list — each (account, metric) pair its own series, invalid points skipped entirely — into the events the cycle write will store. §12.1's condition 1 (usage decrease / remaining increase "beyond the allowed rounding tolerance") is judged jointly across every field pair both points carry: the reset hypothesis predicts a direction per field (usage falls, remaining rises), at least one carried pair must move beyond its tolerance that way, and any carried pair moving beyond its tolerance against the prediction vetoes the event — whether it contradicts its own signal (`percentUsed` rising against a falling `used`) or the other one (`remaining` falling against a falling `used`, which under the provider's checked `used + remaining = limit` arithmetic is a plan re-statement, not a replenishment; found in review). The tolerances are pinned at absolute 0 (every monotonic quantization preserves ordering, so an apparent decrease is always real) and 1 percentage point (the provider reports integer percentages — D-047's documented one-point noise class). Condition 2 fires when the previous point's declared `resetsAt` has passed at the new snapshot and a strictly later one replaced it, and the event time follows the spec: the previous `resetsAt` when it lies strictly between the snapshots, else the new collection time. The conservative approach is the task's done-condition: for `ROLLING_WINDOW` metrics the value condition is inadmissible on its own — a replenishing window can shed any amount between two samples, down to a full drop when a burst exits — so only a declared reset-time advancement records an event, with `rolling_window_conservative` appended to the stored reason; ambiguous evidence of every other kind (out-of-order points, a `limitType`/`unit` redefinition, contradictory fields) records nothing, while contract violations (an invalid point reaching the pair comparison, mismatched accounts or metric keys) fail fast. The output is the D-046 evidence vocabulary and is structurally identical to `CycleEventInput`, so task 118 persists it with no conversion; example and property suites pin both sides of the done-condition (drastic rolling transitions without declaration evidence never fire; every strict fixed-period usage decrease does) and the fold against a group-first oracle over interleaved accounts, metrics, and validity.
- Collection-gap detection (spec §12.2) in `src/services/history-gaps.ts` (task 119, decision D-075): `detectCollectionGaps(points, intervalSource)` is the pure §12.2 rule — a gap is the interval between two adjacent **successful** metric points of the same account and metric whose elapsed time exceeds three polling intervals — with the interpretations the spec leaves open pinned. The threshold is **jitter-expanded**: §7.1 schedules each poll at the configured interval plus an additive jitter of up to 10% (the scheduler's `pollingDelayMs` shape, D-066), so three fully-stretched polls legitimately span 3 × 1.1 intervals; the effective interval is `effectivePollingIntervalMs(seconds)` (exact integer ms — 1100 ms per configured second, derived from `POLLING_JITTER_FRACTION` so the allowance cannot drift from the delays the scheduler actually produces), the threshold is `gapThresholdMs(seconds)` = 3 × that, and "exceeds" is strict — 900 000 ms at the 300 s default is _not_ a gap, 990 000 ms is not, 990 001 ms is. "The `interval_s` in effect at the relevant collection time" resolves at each pair's earlier point, **widened to the span's most permissive cadence when a change lands between the two points**: the interval arrives as a constant or an explicit change timeline (a constant is exactly a one-entry timeline; a change at a point's timestamp applies to it; unsorted timelines are sorted; every point must be covered — a point older than the first change fails fast), so pairs are judged by the cadence they started under, and a straddling pair — whose schedule D-066's `setIntervalMs` re-anchors mid-span, making a runtime grow beyond 2× (60 s → 300 s is one §6.4 edit away) legitimately span up to 1.1 × (old + new) — resolves fail-safe by the widest interval the span ever ran under instead of minting a false gap on a healthy series (a code-review finding against the first draft). Accounts and metrics never share a gap (nested-map partitioning, deterministic output order: account first appearance, then metric, then time); empty and single-point series return nothing, and no gap is invented beyond the first or last observed point (silence with no records is §12.2's stopped-service class, task 120). The done-condition — tests covering interval changes and irregular poll times — is met by `test/unit/history-gaps.spec.ts` (both boundary sides at the raw 3× and jitter-expanded 3× thresholds, cadences inside the jitter bounds, mid-series changes in both directions, straddling pairs in both directions including the re-anchored grow scenario and a straddling silence that still gaps past the widened cadence, an interior change governing the widening, timeline edges, interleaved multi-account/multi-series input, unsorted points and timelines, duplicate timestamps, and the full fail-fast validation table) and `test/unit/history-gaps.property.spec.ts` (scheduler-producible cadences never gap at any §7.1 interval, one stretched delta is exactly one correctly-placed gap, the boundary is exact at every interval, a scheduler-producible runtime grow or shrink never gaps the straddling pair, and arbitrary multi-series input agrees with an independent oracle independent of input order — task 124 extends this suite).
- Last-valid-value queries (task 122, spec §7.2, §11.2; decision D-077) on the storage facade: `QuotaRepository.getLatestValidMetricValues(accountId)` returns each metric key's most recent **valid** row with its own collection time — §7.2's "retain and display the latest valid values and their collection time after an error", generalized past task 110's narrow seam proof (a failed run owns no metric rows at all) to §8.4's per-metric validity. A later invalid row for one metric (NULL numerics, D-010) neither erases that metric's last valid measurement nor surfaces as one, and it never holds back the valid siblings of its snapshot — different metrics' last-valid values may come from different snapshots, each carrying its snapshot-pinned `collected_at` (D-039; the D-070 `lastSuccessAt` precedent for which clock the answer reads). Ordering is `collected_at DESC, run_id DESC` (equal stamps resolve to the later run), the output is `metric_key`-ascending, an account with no valid rows answers an empty array — absence, never zero (§7.2) — and failed runs (parse failures with their §7.4 payloads, network, auth) are invisible by structure (D-036: no snapshot, no metric rows). One window-function statement served by the §11.2 `metric_values_series_access` index (proven via `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN`); the integration suite in `test/integration/repository.spec.ts` pins the full done-condition: happy path, unchanged-after-failures, the no-erase/sibling-updates split across snapshots, invalid→valid recovery, only-invalid/runless/unknown accounts, multi-account isolation, the equal-stamp tiebreak, and the index service.
- Z.ai peak-schedule source verification round (task 127, spec §9.1/§18) appended to `docs/providers/zai-coding-plan.md`: the official schedule/rate pages were re-read on 2026-10-31 (`docs.z.ai/devpack/overview`, `/devpack/teamplan`, the 2026-07-30 usage-revision notice, the documentation index, the devpack FAQ, and the model release notes), and the section stores the complete evidence — URLs, access date, weekdays (Monday–Friday), window (14:00–18:00), time zone (Singapore Standard Time, UTC+8 → `Asia/Singapore`), and rate semantics: on the current credits plans (individual Lite/Pro/Max and team Standard/Premium Seat) peak usage is charged at 1× and off-peak at 0.5× ("50% of the standard credit rate") of the standard credit rate, both multipliers now quoted verbatim ("charged at 1×/0.5× the standard credit rate"); the legacy plan V2 "1×/3×" and "0.4×/1.2×" request multipliers are explicitly fenced off, and every time-limited offer the docs do state (team overage 10% discount, the 50% migration subscription discount, referral credits, Quota Reset Cards) is recorded as an unrelated mechanism — so the documentation describes neither a stale promotion nor a guessed multiplier. The seeded example configuration (task 112) matches every checked value; no change is proposed.
- The peak-hours schedule validation matrix (spec §9.2; task 113, decision D-071) in `test/unit/peak-hours-validation.spec.ts`: every invalid dimension of `providers.<id>.peakHours` — a non-IANA or offset `timezone`, out-of-enum/duplicate/empty `days`, malformed `HH:mm` times (including `24:00` as a start and minutes past `24:00` as an end), inverted/equal/midnight-crossing `start`/`end`, zero, negative, and non-finite multipliers (window and off-peak), same-day overlaps (pair, containment, chain), and duplicate window ids — is pinned to its exact field-level problem through `parseConfig`, the pipeline startup and `quotameter reload` run; the boundaries are pinned accepted (the §9.2 sample and the verified Z.ai schedule verbatim with canonical output, the `24:00` end including full-day `00:00–24:00` and touching windows, identical times on disjoint days, the empty `windows` "not configured" shape, and multiplier defaults), and a fast-check property proves overlap is flagged exactly when two generated windows share a day and their minute-intervals intersect. The rules themselves landed with the configuration contract (061/064, D-014/D-018) — the audit found no missing rule; the matrix consolidates the proof and closes the previously untested cells.
- Account status derivation (spec §7.3, §14.6, §13) in `src/services/polling-status.ts` (task 111, decision D-070): `deriveAccountStatus(input, nowEpochMs)` computes the primary displayed status — `Fresh`, `Collecting`, `Unreachable`, `Auth rejected`, `Stale`, `Format changed`, plus `Disabled` and `Not configured` as separate states — from exactly the evidence the landed pipeline already produces: task 105's `STATUS_CONTRIBUTION_BY_OUTCOME` for the three failure ranks (no re-derivation; a 429 or success contributes nothing and falls through to the age states), task 108's D-064 suspension state for `Auth rejected` (the §7.2 pause _is_ the status — an inconclusive verification probe while suspended keeps rank 1, and a latest `auth_error` run covers the restart/release window for one cycle), and the age of the latest success — or of the first attempt when none exists — against three configured intervals (`Collecting` is the no-data state inside that window, `Fresh` at "no older than" inclusive, `Stale` past it). `readPollingRunEvidence(repository, accountId)` is the storage seam: the three D-058 lookups designed for this consumer (latest cycle, latest snapshot's §8.4 collection stamp as the last success, oldest run as the no-success `Stale` anchor), frozen into one evidence object; `now` is always an argument (D-021), the interval validates against the scheduler's §7.1 bounds, and every malformed input fails fast. The done-condition — every state and transition has tests — is met threefold: the unit suite pins each of the eight statuses from defining evidence, both boundary sides on both anchors, the precedence overlaps, and an event-driven transition matrix (disable/lose-key from every state, every failure arrival, recovery, suspension release by key change/manual test/probe success, restart with a lost suspension, aging past the window); the property suite proves totality, the dominance of `Disabled`/`Not configured`/an active suspension, biconditional agreement with D-048's contributions and the three-interval arithmetic, freshness downward-closed in the clock reading, and time-shift invariance; the integration suite derives end-to-end from real `collection_runs`/`snapshots` rows — one run of each of the seven outcomes through its §7.3 status, the snapshot-stamp (never failed-run) last success, the oldest-run anchor under out-of-order arrival, and per-account isolation.
- Cycle persistence through the settle seam (spec §7.2, §11.1) in `src/services/polling-persist.ts` (task 110, decision D-069): `createCycleRecorder` produces one account's scheduler closure pair — `attempt(signal)` stamps the cycle's wall-clock start and makes the raw `fetchSnapshot` call, and `settleCycle(result)` classifies the retried cycle (task 105), persists it through `persistCollectionCycle` (D-060's §11.1 transaction: one `collection_runs` row per cycle with the total attempt count and `retry_after_seconds` from `deferNextPoll`'s decision — seconds only for a deferred 429, `NULL` otherwise — and the snapshot with its metric rows only for a successful one, the §7.4 sanitized payload beside a parse-error run), then returns the classification the engine gates on. Done-condition, both halves, proven end-to-end against a real migrated database: every failure class — network, timeout, 5xx, 401, 429, adapter parse failure with payload, transport parse failure, and a provider-level success whose value failed §8.4 validation (a `parse_error` run without a snapshot) — writes exactly one run row and no snapshot/metric/event rows; and after a success followed by failures, the latest-snapshot/metric-series/latest-success reads still return the last valid values with their collection time, never zeroed. A caller abort classifies `'cancelled'` and is deliberately unpersisted (service lifecycle, §12.2's no-record class); an error outside both taxonomies throws and is contained per §7.2. The adapter parse failure is recognized structurally (the layout forbids services importing the adapter island), and D-067's raw-payload access gate gained exactly one sanctioned file for this write-side §7.4 composer — the read path stays confined to storage and `doctor --raw`.
- Rolling range boundary calculations (spec §10, §13, §14.4; task 116, decision D-068) in `src/util/rolling-ranges.ts`: the closed §14.4 vocabulary — `24h`, `3d`, `7d` (default), `14d`, `30d`, typed as a union with exact millisecond durations — and `rollingRangeBounds(range, now)` computing the explicit `from`/`to` bounds §10 requires ("last N hours/days" is a rolling interval ending now: `to = now`, `from = now − duration` in pure UTC epoch-ms duration arithmetic, no calendar/DST wall-clock drift). `now` is always an argument (the D-021 clock discipline), so the bounds are identical under any `TZ` setting by construction — the task's done-condition is verified by `test/integration/rolling-ranges-tz.spec.ts`, which runs the real module in child processes under `TZ` = UTC, Europe/Berlin, America/New_York, Asia/Kolkata, Asia/Kathmandu, Pacific/Kiritimati, and no `TZ` at all, asserting byte-identical bounds across all of them while their `Intl`-resolved zones genuinely differ. The bounds form a closed interval (both ends inclusive — the spec is silent on inclusivity; pinned and made executable through `rollingRangeContains` for the Stage 12 server-side filter). Unknown range ids and non-finite `now` values fail loudly with English errors.
- The polling scheduler (spec §7.1, §7.2, §5.6) in `src/services/polling-scheduler.ts` (task 109, decision D-066): `createPollingScheduler` runs §7.1's collection loop as one sequential dispatch engine over per-account state — the first poll for every account fires immediately after startup, inter-poll delays are the configured interval plus a uniform jitter of 0–10% of it (`pollingDelayMs`, with the 60–3600 s §7.1 range validated at assembly and drift-tested against the config contract), dues are measured on the monotonic clock, and per-account overlap is structural (an account with a live cycle is never dispatched; one cycle starts at a time). A cycle unsettled past the hang threshold (default 255.5 s — §7.2's worst legitimate cycle of four 60 s attempts plus the 15.5 s worst backoff) is skipped so one hanging closure cannot starve other accounts, while the hung account itself still never overlaps. The §7.2 gates compose the landed seams as data: `deferNextPoll`'s `notBeforeEpochMs` defers the next poll (every dispatch, manual tests included) and the D-064 state machine paces hourly verification probes (`admitVerification`), with releases by key change (reported through `setAccounts`), manual test (`requestManualTest`), or a successful probe. Each account is an injectable closure pair — the raw `attempt` (wrapped in `runCycleWithRetries`, D-059) and `settleCycle`, the task-110 classify+persist seam whose returned classification drives all scheduling; a throwing seam is contained per §7.2's one-account-failure rule, a key change mid-flight discards the stale cycle's outcome, and a wiring-level loop failure is observable (`running` turns false, `stop()` re-surfaces it). `stop()` is §5.6's stop-scheduling: aborts every live cycle and resolves promptly, with an optional D-020 in-flight tracker for the graceful drain; every wake is sliced to one hour (`MAX_WAKE_MS`) so a wall-regressed gate wait can never busy-spin. Fake-timer/concurrency tests (`test/unit/polling-scheduler.spec.ts`) pin immediacy, sequencing, both jitter boundaries, the hourly probe cadence, mid-run account/interval changes, overlap impossibility under a hanging closure, clean shutdown, and the review-round regression pins (mid-flight re-key discard, the owed manual attempt, loop-death observability, a 40-day wall regression, the loud broken-sleep death, a throwing tracker `done()`); the shared `withFakeTimers` helper gained an additive `now` option for exact absolute-timestamp assertions.
- Raw-response retention and CLI-only access (spec §7.4, §5.5) in `src/storage/raw-response-retention.ts` and `src/cli/raw-diagnostics.ts` (task 103, decision D-067): `enforceRawResponseCap(repository, accountId)` keeps an account's newest 20 sanitized parse-failure payloads (`RAW_RESPONSE_MAX_PER_ACCOUNT` — fixed policy, the count twin of D-063's 30-day constant) and evicts the rest by run id, composing the two D-058 primitives designed for it; `persistCollectionCycle` enforces it inside the cycle's own transaction whenever it stores a §7.4 payload, so after **every** committed parse-failure cycle the account holds at most 20 — continuously, not on a cleanup cadence (a daily pass would retain hundreds of rows between passes at §7.1's polling floor), and the eviction is atomic with the write (an injected failure rolls the whole cycle back). Eviction deletes payloads only — the runs' `collection_runs` rows survive — and one pass repairs any facade-seeded overshoot. `readRawDiagnostics`/`renderRawDiagnostics` are the diagnostic read path behind the now-declared `doctor --raw <account>` option (the handler stays a stub until task 145): newest-first, bounded by the cap, body verbatim, timestamps in ISO 8601 UTC. The "never over HTTP or in the web UI" half of the done-condition is forward-enforceable, not just today-true: `test/unit/raw-response-gate.spec.ts` (the D-058 SQL-gate pattern) fails `make check` on any `src/**`/`ui/**` file outside `src/storage/**` and `src/cli/**` that references the raw-payload surface — facade identifiers or the diagnostic module's own API/import path — so the first Stage 12 route or UI component to reach for stored payloads breaks the build instead of shipping.
- Authorization-rejection suspension (spec §7.2, §7.3) in `src/services/polling-suspension.ts` (task 108, decision D-064): a pure, injectable-time state machine that pauses an account's normal polling after a 401/403 cycle — until the key changes or a manual test is requested — and admits exactly one verification attempt per 60 minutes. `applyCycleOutcome` enters or re-arms suspension on `auth_error` (re-arm to the rejection + 60 min, never earlier), releases on `success`, and pins every other outcome as a no-op (an inconclusive probe proves nothing about the key); `applyKeyIdentity` is the key-replacement release event (the caller reports an opaque key identity; the machine compares identities, never key material); `applyManualTestRequest` is the manual-test release event. `admitVerification` is the gate the polling scheduler (109) requests automatic attempts through: granted only at/after the armed instant (exactly 60 minutes admits), re-armed a full window at admission, monotone within an episode, and carried across releases as an admission floor — so no rapid retry loop is possible under any caller behavior, including lost outcomes, duplicate rejections, and wall-clock regressions. The property suite (`test/unit/polling-suspension.property.spec.ts`) is the standing proof: generated adversarial sequences never admit two attempts closer than the cadence (with vacuity tripwires guaranteeing the walks produce admissions), a directed release-and-regress property pins the admission floor — release, then a rejection stamped below the admission it follows, must not re-open the gate for the rest of the window — and admission agrees with the armed instant at every probe time.
- Migration backups and newer-schema refusal (spec §5.6, §11.4) in `src/storage/`: `runStartupMigrations({ database, dataDir, migrations, clock? })` in `startup-migrations.ts` is §11.4's startup operation the server wiring will call — it reads the schema-version relation first (read-only) and refuses a newer schema by throwing `NewerSchemaError` (message names both versions, the newer journal entries, and §5.6's `npm update -g quotameter` remedy) **before any backup, migration, or runner construction**, so the refused database is byte-identical and no backup directory appears; an up-to-date database returns without touching anything; a behind database holding a pre-migration state is backed up before the D-027 harness applies pending migrations, while an empty fresh install skips the backup — there is no pre-migration state to protect, and the carve-out is emptiness, not the journal: a journal-less but non-empty database (a foreign file, a lost `schema_migrations`) is still backed up (decision D-065). `createAutomaticBackup({ database, backupDir, now })` in `backups.ts` is the `VACUUM INTO` primitive: a standalone image (committed-but-uncheckpointed WAL content included, pinned by test on a WAL connection) written to `<data>/backups/backup-<epoch-ms>[-<seq>].sqlite` with the filename bound as a parameter (never interpolated), a `-<seq>` suffix disambiguating same-instant names (SQLite refuses an existing target), retention keeping exactly three by parsed `(timestamp, seq)` order — never a lexicographic sort, which inverts both `backup-T` against `backup-T-1` and digit-width boundaries (999 vs 1000) — and evicting only after the new backup exists, never the just-created backup itself (a clock step-back or same-instant revisit must not delete the image its own pass exists to protect; review finding), and only files matching the automatic naming grammar (operator files are untouched). The done-condition is verified by `test/integration/startup-migrations.spec.ts`: forward migration with a usable backup (opens independently, integrity ok, carries the pre-migration schema and data), retention (exactly three kept, oldest evicted), newer-schema refusal (database byte-identical), and interrupted-migration recovery (backup and pre-migration state survive a failing migration; a corrected re-run completes) (task 092).
- Retention cleanup and storage maintenance (spec §11.3, §7.4, §15.2) in `src/storage/`: `runRetentionCleanup({ repository, database }, { now, retentionDays })` in `retention-cleanup.ts` is the §11.3 operation as one repeat-safe call — it deletes every run started before `now − retentionDays` (the cascade removes its snapshot, metric values, and linked events/raw payload), every event older than that cutoff, every raw payload older than §7.4's fixed 30-day window (the time-based half of task 103's policy; the 20-per-account cap stays 103's), and every session expired at `now` per §15.2 — all in one transaction that also writes the §11.2 cleanup metadata (`retention:last-run`, `retention:applied-days` — the recorded hook the future config/UI reduce-retention warning reads), then runs the maintenance primitive. `runStorageMaintenance(database)` in `maintenance.ts` is that primitive: `wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE)` (its `busy` outcome is reported data, not an error) followed by `incremental_vacuum` with the database's `auto_vacuum` mode in the report — a documented no-op on the service's `NONE` default, real work on operator-configured `INCREMENTAL` files; the §5.6 shutdown wiring can reuse it. Inputs are writer-supplied (D-021 — the scheduler owns `now`); `retentionDays` outside §11.3's 7–3650 integer range is rejected before any delete runs; accounts (active and archived), configuration, and unrelated meta are untouched by construction. The done-condition — startup and daily cleanup are safe to repeat — is verified by test: an identical second run removes nothing, a later `now` removes only the newly expired, an earlier one is a no-op, and an injected marker-write failure rolls every delete back (decision D-063; task 091).
- `Retry-After` scheduling (spec §7.2) in `src/services/polling-retry-after.ts` (task 107, decision D-062): after a 429 cycle — which task 106's wrapper already leaves un-retried — `deferNextPoll(classified, nowEpochMs)` defers the account's next poll to the provider-indicated time, capped at one hour, and `parseRetryAfter(raw, nowEpochMs)` is the pure RFC 9110 parser underneath (delta-seconds or HTTP-date in all three accepted formats, with §5.6.7's sliding 50-year rule for RFC 850 two-digit years, HTTP-whitespace-only trimming, time injected per D-021). The failure modes are decisions, not crashes: malformed values produce no deferral (the normal interval, `NULL` in the run row), past HTTP-dates clamp to zero seconds, and excessive values — including magnitudes no double can represent — cap at 3600 s with a `capped` flag; date quantization rounds up so the wait never ends before the indicated instant. Only a 429 defers (task 105's `retryAfterEligible` gate lives inside the entry point, so a 503 carrying the header is retried in-cycle instead); the polling scheduler (109) gates on `notBeforeEpochMs` and the run writer (110) persists `seconds` — both within the range the `collection_runs_retry_after_range` CHECK already enforces.
- Account archival and history purge (spec §8.2, §11.2) in `src/storage/account-lifecycle.ts`: `retireAccount` is the default retirement — the account is archived and stays queryable with every history row intact, re-retirement is a no-op keeping the original timestamp (D-058's archive-once semantic), and the outcome is disambiguated (`unknown`/`archived`/`already-archived`) for the API's 404-versus-idempotent-success distinction; `purgeAccount` is the separate explicit action — the account row is deleted (active or archived alike, no archive-first requirement) and the cascading foreign keys remove its entire history (runs, snapshots, metric values, events, raw payloads) while sessions and meta survive as service state (D-056), with a per-table evidence report of what was removed and a post-delete residue sweep that fails loudly if the cascade left anything behind (the foreign-keys-off connection defense). Archive and purge have distinct test suites verifying the cascade across every history table, cross-account isolation, the archived-account queryability guarantee, and the residue failure (decision D-061; task 090).
- Within-cycle retry/backoff (spec §7.2) in `src/services/polling-retry.ts` (task 106, decision D-059): `runCycleWithRetries` wraps one collection-cycle attempt and retries network failures, timeouts, and HTTP 5xx up to three times with the exponential 2/4/8 s delays plus a uniform bounded jitter of 0–500 ms — eligibility delegated in full to task 105's `classifyTransportError().retryable`, so 429, 401/403, and parse defects report immediately with a single attempt. The result object `{ ok, value | error, attempts }` never throws for provider failures and carries the total attempt count (1–4) that `collection_runs.attempts` will persist; a caller abort interrupts the backoff and surfaces as `TransportAbortError` (the `'cancelled'` pseudo-outcome); the per-attempt 15 s request timeout stays with the transport, a timed-out attempt being simply one more retryable failure. Randomness and sleep are injectable seams; fake-timer tests prove the counts and exact delay/jitter boundaries, and integration tests pin eligibility and attempts against the mock provider server over real sockets.
- Atomic collection-cycle persistence (spec §11.1) in `src/storage/collection-cycles.ts`: `persistCollectionCycle(repository, cycle)` writes the run row every cycle leaves behind — successful or not — and, in one `withTransaction` unit, the snapshot, its metric rows, and its reset events (a parse-failure cycle carries its §7.4 sanitized payload beside the run instead; other failures carry only run evidence). The writer supplies every identity field — the cycle's account, the assigned run id, the snapshot's collection time — so metric/event/payload inputs cannot express D-039 denormalization drift or a wrong-account event. Any failure — a named CHECK, a UNIQUE collision, or a driver-level throw — rolls the whole cycle back and propagates unchanged; the done-condition ("injected failures leave no partial snapshot") is verified by failures injected at every insert position, each asserting zero residue (decision D-060; task 089).
- Typed repository facade `QuotaRepository` (spec §8.1/§11) in `src/storage/repository.ts`: synchronous CRUD/query methods over every §11.2 table plus `meta` — accounts (insert/get/list/update/archive-once/cascade-purge), collection runs (insert returning the assigned id, latest-by-outcome for §7.3 status, filtered history), snapshots (success-only by structure — the composite FK makes an error cycle unable to create one), metric values (the §8.4 persisted model with `derived_fields` bitmask encode/decode and the `metric_values_series_access`-served series query), quota events (§12.1 evidence JSON round-trip), raw responses (parse-error-only by structure, newest-first bounded listing), sessions (digest lookup, joint last-seen/expiry refresh, single/all/expired deletes), and meta key/value upsert — with `withTransaction` as the §11.1 atomicity seam task 089 composes, writer-supplied timestamps everywhere, and the named CHECK constraints as the write gate (decision D-058). The task-088 done-condition is mechanically enforced: `test/unit/storage-sql-gate.spec.ts` fails the build on any uppercase house-style SQL statement literal outside `src/storage/` (a tripwire against accidental ad hoc SQL complementing the existing `node:sqlite` import gate — deliberately not a security boundary, D-058); `test/integration/repository.spec.ts` pins the surface against the canonical schema.
- Property-based derived-field audit (task 101, spec §8.4/§17.1) in `test/unit/zai-coding-plan-mapping.property.spec.ts`: the done-condition — percentages are never used to invent token counts — is proven over every recognized identity and arbitrary finite percentages (no absolute fields, `unit: PERCENT`, verbatim `percentUsed`, `percentRemaining = 100 − percentUsed`, `derivedFields` exactly `['percentRemaining']`), and a generated field-subset oracle (exact and provably violated triples, arbitrary field presence, IEEE-754 extremes always in play) holds every derivation path to its exact firing condition, exact value, `derivedFields` recording, the no-chaining rule, and the shared snapshot invariants. Example additions pin the overflow guards, the window-scaling overflow, a lone allowance, and a derived `remaining` beside a supplied percentage.
- `sessions` and `meta` tables (spec §11.2/§15.2) as canonical migration `0007-sessions-meta` in `src/storage/migrations.ts` — the last §11.2 tables, in one entry: sessions store only the SHA-256 hex digest of the cookie token as their identity (an exact 64-lowercase-hex-char CHECK — the raw token's non-hex wire forms cannot fit and the pinned column set admits no raw/cookie/secret column; D-056 records the non-hex cookie-encoding precondition task 154 must keep, a hex cookie being shape-identical to the digest), with `created`/`last-seen`/effective-expiry timestamps and both §15.2 bounds CHECK-enforced (`expires_at <= created + 30 d` absolute, `expires_at <= last_seen + 7 d` inactivity, durations exported as `SESSION_MAX_AGE_MS`/`SESSION_INACTIVITY_TIMEOUT_MS`); the spec-named `sessions_expiry` index proven used via query plans for §11.3's expired-session cleanup. `meta` is the exact key/value service-state table (non-empty key, any TEXT value) for task 091's cleanup metadata. Neither table is account-bound — purging accounts leaves both untouched (decision D-056).
- Z.ai metric-mapping verification round (task 100, spec §8.3/§8.4): every D-047 mapping row is now contract-tested in `test/unit/zai-coding-plan-mapping.spec.ts` — each `(type, unit)` recognition identity pinned by exact full-object equality (labels, field presence and absence, including no `windowSeconds` on calendar windows, `windowSeconds = number × 3600` scaled by the supplied `number`, no absolutes on percentage-only entries, and no `usageDetails` leakage), a unit-discipline matrix over every parseable fixture body (closed `unit` enum, `PERCENT` metrics carry no absolute fields, `percentRemaining` always derived with exact percent sums, invariant-clean snapshots), and the previously unpinned tolerance cross-cells (wrong-typed `usageDetails` tolerated, no percentage↔absolutes cross-validation, `percentage: 0` present, `null` value fields wrong-typed, type-strict recognition). No mapping semantics changed; the audit added tests only (verification section appended to the provider dossier).
- Z.ai Coding Plan snapshot collection (task 099, spec §7.2/§8.1/§8.3/§8.4) in `src/providers/zai-coding-plan/`: `fetchSnapshot` runs the dossier's endpoint trio through the shared transport (endpoint 1 primary with envelope validation and the D-047 metric mapping into the §8.4 model; endpoints 2/3 fetched softly for plan-name context, their failure never fails the cycle), the §8.1 abort signal composed into every exchange, and parse failures throwing `SnapshotParseError` with the §7.4 sanitized-payload hand-off (decision D-053); the mock provider server helper gained exact-match per-path behaviors for multi-endpoint cycles.
- Z.ai Coding Plan credential validation (task 098, spec §8.1/§8.3) in `src/providers/zai-coding-plan/`: the adapter island's `validateCredentials` performs one Bearer GET to the dossier's endpoint 4 (`GET {coding-base}/models`, default `https://api.z.ai/api/coding/paas/v4/models`) through the shared transport and maps outcomes status-based into three distinct results — resolve on a delivered 2xx, `CredentialsRejectedError` on HTTP 401/403 (assumption A3), `ProviderUnavailableError` on any other transport failure — with the response body never parsed and the account key never echoed in any error (decision D-052).
- Synthetic Z.ai response matrix (task 097, spec §8.3/§17.1) under `test/fixtures/synthetic/`: nineteen fixtures covering the adapter-contract rows the single sanitized capture cannot — the weekly `unit 6` entry, absolute-value (`CREDITS`) entries, partial/duplicate/invalid/renamed/extra-field/different-plan/different-unit limit responses, envelope success spellings and failure shapes, wrong-typed containers, divergent subscription record selection, non-empty reset cards, and the never-captured `/models` body. Every file is registered with its origin (invented or assumption-shaped per the 096 ledger A1/A2/A4/A5) and the contract rows it serves in `test/fixtures/README.md`; deliberately unfixtured rows (HTTP-level failures, trivial cross-cells) are recorded there too (decision D-049).
- Z.ai adapter contract (task 096, spec §8.3/§8.4/§7.2) appended to `docs/providers/zai-coding-plan.md`: the implementation-facing requests (the verified endpoint trio plus the status-based credential-check endpoint), response-envelope rules, the field-by-field metric mapping into the §8.4 model with pinned value semantics (`usage` = allowance, `currentValue` = consumed, `percentage` = integer percent), stable metric identities, per-endpoint failure policy for collection cycles, and an assumption ledger where every unverified gap carries its chosen adapter behavior and falsifier (decision D-047).
- `docs/mutation-testing.md`: how the daily mutation workflow runs, how to inspect runs and artifacts, and the triage rules for survivors and below-threshold reports (D-045).
- Provider error classification (spec §7.2, §7.3, §11.2) in `src/services/polling-outcomes.ts`: transport failures and adapter parse outcomes map to the seven-value `collection_runs` outcome enum with retained HTTP status, raw `Retry-After` passthrough (429-only eligibility), §7.2 within-cycle retryability, and the §7.3 precedence-ordered status contributions; caller aborts classify as an unpersistable `'cancelled'` pseudo-outcome (decision D-048).
- `raw_responses` table (spec §7.4/§11.2) as canonical migration `0006-raw-responses` in `src/storage/migrations.ts`: one bounded sanitized parse-failure payload per run — "one per run" structural via a `run_id` UNIQUE column, the parse-error-only linkage (never a success run, never another account's run) structural via a composite foreign key over the existing `collection_runs (account_id, id, outcome)` parent key with a CHECK-bound `parse_error` carrier, and the D-041 64 KiB cap enforced in SQL as `octet_length(body) <= 65536` (bytes, not characters — at-cap passes, multibyte over-cap rejected), with the storage-side constant drift-tested against the sanitizer's; the `raw_responses_account_time` index proven used via query plans for task 103's per-account retention scans (decision D-051).

### Changed

- Mutation testing moved out of `make check-all` and per-push CI into a daily scheduled workflow (`.github/workflows/mutation.yml`) that upserts a rolling `mutation-report` issue with score, per-file table, and survived-mutant triage list; `make mutation` still runs it manually (decision D-045).

### Added

- MIT `LICENSE` and attribution `NOTICE` files.
- Project documentation: technical specification, implementation task plan, decision log, contributing guide.
- Docker-based development environment pinned to Node.js 22.13.0 with isolated data volumes.
- Testing foundation: Vitest (unit, integration, component projects), Playwright E2E smoke and accessibility checks, fast-check property tests, local mock provider server, no-network guard.
- Quality gates: ESLint with security and i18n rules, strict TypeScript for server and UI, Prettier formatting, coverage thresholds, Stryker mutation testing.
- CI pipeline: Node.js matrix, Ubuntu/Debian clean-install containers, tarball allowlist check, unsupported-Node behavior test, Dependabot.
- Bootstrap `quotameter` CLI binary reporting the package version with a runtime version gate.
- Application package layout: layered `src/` modules (schemas, util, config, providers, storage, services, server, cli) with documented dependency rules enforced by a structural import-direction test.
- Server bootstrap: process entry point with a runtime gate (Node.js >= 22.13.0, non-root), signal-driven startup/shutdown, and the application lifecycle skeleton in `src/server/`.
- Normalized snapshot and metric model (spec §8.4) in `src/schemas/metrics.ts`: closed `LimitType`/`MetricUnit`/derived-field enums with runtime guards, `ProviderSnapshot`/`NormalizedMetric` types, and spec invariant checks shared by server, storage, API, and UI.
